Appendix B
Proofs for Section 5

In this section, we prove that the results of
Algorithm

1
and
Algorithm

2
are what we desire. For instance, we show that the language
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
recognized by the output automaton
\mathcal{A}^{R}
of
Algorithm

1
on the input automaton
\mathcal{A}
and quantum gate
\mathrm{U}\in\{\mathrm{X}_{t},\mathrm{Y}_{t},\mathrm{Z}_{t},\mathrm{S}_{t},\mathrm{T}_{t}\}
(for some
t\in[n]
) is exactly the language
\{\mathrm{U}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\}
. Intuitively,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})=\{\mathrm{U}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\}
if and only if for each
T^{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
, we can find
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\mathrm{U}(T)=T^{R}
and for each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
there is
T^{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
such that
T^{R}=\mathrm{U}(T)
. Instead of showing this directly, we prove that
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
are one-to-one correspondent by giving a constructive proof, i.e., we build a bijective mapping
\mathcal{U}_{t}
from
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
, where
\mathcal{U}_{t}
, indeed, maps
T
to
T^{R}
.

Lemma B.1 (Constant Scaling).

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\Delta=\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{l},\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a TA representing certain set of
n
-qubit states. For any given
t\in[n]
and
a_{0},a_{1}
such that
\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&0\\
0&a_{1}\end{pmatrix}
being unitary, let
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\langle Q^{\prime},\Sigma,\Delta_{1},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\rangle
and
\mathcal{A}^{R}=\langle Q^{R},\Sigma^{R}=\Sigma,\Delta^{R},\mathcal{R}^{R}=\mathcal{R}\rangle
be the resulting TAs under the corresponding procedure from line 2 to line 4 in the Algorithm
1
, where

\displaystyle:=\{q^{\prime}\mid q\in Q\},

\displaystyle\Delta^{R}

\displaystyle:=\Delta^{\mathrm{new}}\cup\Delta_{1}.

Then, we have
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})=\{\mathrm{U}_{t}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\}
, where
\mathrm{U}_{t}=\mathrm{Id}\otimes\cdots\mathrm{Id}\otimes\mathrm{U}\otimes\mathrm{Id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{Id}
whose
t
-th component
\mathrm{U}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&0\\
0&a_{1}\end{pmatrix}
is unitary, and we denote by
\mathrm{U}_{t}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}^{R}
.

Proof.

We are going to construct a map

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{t}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{t}(T)=:T^{R}

such that
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is a bijection. Given a
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with its accepting run
\rho
, since
\Sigma^{R}=\Sigma
, we form a tree
T^{R}
with
N_{T^{R}}=N_{T}
as follows: for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}
we set
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)\in\Sigma^{R}=\Sigma
. Let
\rho^{R}
be the run defined as

(1)

L_{\rho^{R}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)
;

(2)

for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}
with
L_{T}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}\}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
;

(3)

for nodes (including leaf ones)
u=w.0.\tilde{u}
,
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, with
L_{T}(\tilde{u})=x_{t}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
;

(4)

for nodes (including leaf ones)
u=w.1.\tilde{u}
,
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, with
L_{T}(\tilde{u})=x_{t}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)^{\prime}
;

(5)

for each leaf node
u=0.\tilde{u}
or
u=1.\tilde{u}
with an internal node
\tilde{u}
,

L_{T^{R}}(u)=\begin{cases}a_{0}\times L_{T}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho^{R}}(\tilde{u})=L_{\rho}(\tilde{u})\\
a_{1}\times L_{T}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho^{R}}(\tilde{u})=L_{\rho}(\tilde{u})^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Let
u\in N_{T^{R}}
be an internal node. By construction, if
L_{T^{R}}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{t-1}\}
, the transition

L_{\rho^{R}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to50.36pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.18065pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 18.8 12.28 L -18.8 12.28 C -22.24 12.28 -25.03 9.49 -25.03 6.05 L -25.03 0.81 C -25.03 -2.63 -22.24 -5.42 -18.8 -5.42 L 18.8 -5.42 C 22.24 -5.42 25.03 -2.63 25.03 0.81 L 25.03 6.05 C 25.03 9.49 22.24 12.28 18.8 12.28 Z M -25.03 -5.42}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-15.3901pt}{-0.03874pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}{}}}{{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\mathcal{U}_{t}

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
and is clearly one-to-one.

Claim 1:

The map
\mathcal{U}_{t}
is surjective.

Proof:
For each
T_{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
with the accepting run
\rho_{R}
, since
\Sigma=\Sigma^{R}
, we can form a tree
T
such that
N_{T}=N_{T_{R}}
and
L_{T}(u)=L_{T_{R}}(u)\in\Sigma
for each internal node
u\in N_{T}=N_{T_{R}}
. Since
Q^{R}=Q\cup Q^{\prime}
, for each leaf node
u
we set

L_{T}(u)=\begin{cases}a_{0}^{-1}\times L_{T_{R}}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q\\
a_{1}^{-1}\times L_{T_{R}}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q^{\prime}.\end{cases}

To construct the associated run
\rho
, since
\mathcal{R}^{R}=\mathcal{R}\subset Q
, we may set
L_{\rho}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho_{R}}(\epsilon)
. Moreover, since
Q^{R}=Q\cup Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}=\{q^{\prime}\mid q\in Q\}
is in one-to-one correspondence to
Q
, we denote by
(q^{\prime})^{un-primed}
the corresponding
q\in Q
to
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
. Then we may set

(14)

L_{\rho}(u)=\begin{cases}L_{\rho_{R}}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q\\
L_{\rho_{R}}(u)^{un-primed}&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q^{\prime}\end{cases}

as well. Since each transition in
\Delta^{R}
corresponds to one in
\Delta
, the tree
T
constructed above together with the run
\rho
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{U}_{t}(T)=:T^{R}=T_{R}
. Let
\rho^{R}
be the run associated to
T^{R}
. By construction, we have
N_{T^{R}}=N_{T}=N_{T_{R}}
,
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)=L_{T_{R}}(u)
for each internal node
u
,
L_{\rho^{R}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho_{R}}(\epsilon)
and
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)=L_{\rho_{R}}(u)
for internal nodes
u
with
L_{T}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}\}
. If
L_{T}(u)=x_{t}=L_{T_{R}}(u)
, then

L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to50.36pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.18065pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

Therefore
T^{R}=T_{R}
and hence
\mathcal{U}_{t}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
is surjective.

\blacksquare

Thus we have shown that every
T_{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
is of the form
\mathcal{U}_{t}(T)
for certain
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

It is obvious to see that
\mathcal{U}_{t}(T)=\mathrm{U}_{t}(T)
where
\mathrm{U}_{t}=\mathrm{Id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{U}\otimes\mathrm{Id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{Id}
whose
t
-th component
\mathrm{U}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{0}&0\\
0&a_{1}\end{pmatrix}
. For instance, we may without loss of generality assume that
t=n
. Then, for
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with

T=\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}^{n}}c_{i}\left|i\right\rangle,

we have

T^{R}=\sum_{w\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}}\bigg(a_{0}\times c_{0.w}\left|0.w\right\rangle+a_{1}\times c_{1.w}\left|1.w\right\rangle\bigg)=\mathrm{U}_{n}(T)

as desired.

∎

Lemma B.2 (Swapping subtrees).

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\Delta=\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{l},\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a TA representing certain set of
n

t\in[n]

Then,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})=\{\mathrm{X}_{t}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\}
.

Proof.

As before, we are going to construct a map

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{t}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{X}_{t}=:T^{R}.

Given a
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with its accepting run
\rho
, we construct a tree
T^{R}
of the same shape as follows: since
\Sigma^{R}=\Sigma
, we set
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)
for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}=N_{T}
and
L_{\rho^{R}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)
. For the cases that
L_{T}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}\}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
as well. For the rest of nodes (including leaf nodes)
u
, we may write
u=w.0.\tilde{u}
or
u=w.1.\tilde{u}
for some (possibly empty) word
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
with
L_{T}(u)=x_{t}
and we define

L_{\rho^{R}}(w.0.\tilde{u})=L_{\rho}(w.1.\tilde{u})\quad\text{and}\quad L_{\rho^{R}}(w.1.\tilde{u})=L_{\rho}(w.0.\tilde{u})

and for leaf nodes
u=\tilde{w}.0.\tilde{u}
or
u=\tilde{w}.1.\tilde{u}

L_{T^{R}}(\tilde{w}.0.\tilde{u})=L_{T}(\tilde{w}.1.\tilde{u})\quad\text{and}\quad L_{T^{R}}(\tilde{w}.1.\tilde{u})=L_{T}(\tilde{w}.0.\tilde{u}).

Similar to the argument for constant scaling, it is clear that the constructed
T^{R}
together with the run
\rho^{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
. Note that such a map
\mathcal{X}_{t}
is also well-defined on applying to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
with destination
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, namely,
\mathcal{X}_{t}(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R}))\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
. Moreover, we have
\mathcal{X}_{t}(\mathcal{X}_{T}(T))=T
for all
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
. It follows that every element
T_{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
is of the form
\mathcal{X}_{t}(T)
for certain
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
and vice versa. The functionality
\mathcal{X}_{t}(T)=\mathrm{X}_{t}(T)
holds obviously.
∎

Lemma B.3.

Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathcal{A}=\langle Q,\Sigma,\Delta=\Delta_{i}\cup\Delta_{l},\mathcal{R}\rangle
be a TA representing certain set of
n
-qubit states. For any pair
0<c<t\leq n
, let
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\langle Q_{1},\Sigma,\Delta_{1},\mathcal{R}\rangle
be either
\mathrm{X}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
or
\mathrm{Z}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}
be the primed copy of
\mathcal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle\sqcup\Delta_{l}.

Then
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})=\{\mathrm{U}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{A}\}
for
\mathrm{U}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{CNOT}^{c}_{t}&\text{ if }\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathrm{X}_{t}(\mathcal{A})\\
\mathrm{CZ}^{c}_{t}&\text{ if }\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathrm{Z}_{t}(\mathcal{A})\end{cases}
.

Proof.

By above lemmas, there exist a bijection
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})
. For any
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with the accepting run
\rho
, we denote by
\mathcal{V}(T)
and
\mathcal{V}(\rho)
their corresponding image in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})
under the bijective map
\mathcal{V}
. We also denote by
\mathcal{V}(Q)\subset Q_{1}
the subset of states in
Q_{1}
that appear in the accepted languages in
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})
. Thus the map
\mathcal{V}:Q\to\mathcal{V}(Q)
is bijective.

Again we are going to construct a map

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{U}(T)=:T^{R}

to prove the statement. Given a
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with its accepting run
\rho
, we form a tree
T^{R}
such that
N_{T^{R}}=N_{T}
together with a run
\rho^{R}
as follows: since
\Sigma=\Sigma^{R}
, for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}
we set
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)\in\Sigma
and
L_{\rho^{R}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)
since
\mathcal{R}^{R}=\mathcal{R}
. And

(1)

for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}
with
L_{T}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{c}\}
, we simply let
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
;

(2)

for
u
such that
L_{T}(u)=x_{c}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(0.u)=L_{\rho}(0.u)
and
L_{\rho^{R}}(1.u)=L_{\rho}(1.u)^{\prime}
;

(3)

for
u=w.0.\tilde{u}
for
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and
\tilde{u}
with
L_{T}(\tilde{u})=x_{c}
, we set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
;

(4)

similarly for
u=w.1.\tilde{u}
for some non-empty word
w\in\{0,1\}^{*}
and
\tilde{u}
with
L_{T}(\tilde{u})=x_{c}
, set
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\mathcal{V}(\rho)}(u)^{\prime}
;

(5)

for leaf node
u=\tilde{w}.0.\tilde{u}
(resp.
u=\tilde{w}.1.\tilde{u}
), set
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)
(resp.
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{\mathcal{V}(T)}(u)
).

By construction, for each internal node
u\in N_{T^{R}}
, if
L_{T^{R}}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{c-1}\}
, then

L_{\rho^{R}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to50.36pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.18065pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\mathcal{U}
is well-defined, i.e.,
T^{R}
together with
\rho^{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
, and is injective. It remains to prove the following Claim:

Claim 2:

The map
\mathcal{U}
is surjective.

Proof:
For each
T_{R}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
with its accepting run
\rho_{R}
, since
\Sigma=\Sigma^{R}
, we can form a tree
T
such that
N_{T}=N_{T_{R}}
and
L_{T}(u)=L_{T_{R}}(u)
for all internal nodes
u\in N_{T}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{V}(Q)^{\prime}\subset Q_{1}^{\prime}
is in one-to-one correspondence to
Q
, we denote by
(\mathcal{V}^{-1}(q^{\prime}))^{un-primed}
the corresponding
q\in Q
to
\mathcal{V}(q)^{\prime}\in Q_{1}^{\prime}
. Then we may set

L_{\rho}(u)=\begin{cases}L_{\rho_{R}}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q\\
(\mathcal{V}^{-1}(L_{\rho_{R}}(u)))^{un-primed}&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\in Q_{1}^{\prime}\end{cases}

as well. Since each transition in
\Delta^{R}
corresponds to one in
\Delta
, the tree
T
constructed above together with the run
\rho
belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
.

To show that
T^{R}:=\mathcal{U}(T)=T_{R}
, let
\rho^{R}
be the run associated to
T^{R}
. By construction, we have
N_{T^{R}}=N_{T}=N_{T_{R}}
,
L_{T^{R}}(u)=L_{T}(u)=L_{T_{R}}(u)
for each internal node
u
,
L_{\rho^{R}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho_{R}}(\epsilon)
and
L_{\rho^{R}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)=L_{\rho_{R}}(u)
for internal nodes
u
with
L_{T}(u)\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{c}\}
. If
L_{T}(u)=x_{c}=L_{T_{R}}(u)
, then

L_{\rho_{R}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to50.36pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.18065pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

. In the former cases, we have always

(15)

Therefore
T^{R}=T_{R}
and hence
\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{R})
is surjective.

\blacksquare

The functionality is obvious.

∎

Theorem B.4 (Theorem 5.1, 5.2 and 5.3).

\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{U}(\mathcal{A}))=\{\mathrm{U}(T)\mid T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\}
for

\mathrm{U}\in\{\mathrm{X}_{t},\mathrm{Y}_{t},\mathrm{Z}_{t},\mathrm{S}_{t},\mathrm{T}_{t},\mathrm{CNOT}^{c}_{t},\mathrm{CZ}^{c}_{t},\mathrm{Toffoli}^{c,c^{\prime}}_{t}\}.

Proof.

For
\mathrm{U}\in\{\mathrm{Z}_{t},\mathrm{S}_{t},\mathrm{T}_{t}\}
, the statement follows from Lemma
B.1
, for
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{X}_{t}
, the statement follows from Lemma
B.2
and for
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Y}_{t}
it follows from Lemma
B.1
and Lemma
B.2
combined. For
\mathrm{U}\in\{\mathrm{CNOT}^{c}_{t},\mathrm{CZ}^{c}_{t}\}
it follows from Lemma
B.3
.

Finally, for
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Toffoli}^{c,c^{\prime}}_{t}
, we may set
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathrm{CNOT}^{c^{\prime}}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
. Since
0<c<c^{\prime}<t\leq n
and there exists a bijection
\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{CNOT}^{c^{\prime}}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
by above. Following line by line in the proof of Lemma
B.3
with
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathrm{CNOT}^{c^{\prime}}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
, we deduce the statement for
\mathrm{U}=\mathrm{Toffoli}^{c,c^{\prime}}_{t}
.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs for Section 6

Similar to the previous section, in this one we provide the proofs for Theorems and Lemmas in Section 6.

Lemma C.1 (Lemma 6.3).

All non-single-valued trees in a tagged TA have different tags.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a tagged TA. Suppose that
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
have the same tag, say

\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{2})=x_{1}^{i_{1}}(x_{2}^{i_{2}}(\cdots(x_{n}^{i_{m}}(\square,\square),x_{n}^{i_{l}}(\square,\square)),\cdots))),

and let
\rho_{1}
(resp.
\rho_{2}
) be the corresponding accepting run of
T_{1}
(resp.
T_{2}
). Note that since
\mathcal{A}
is deterministic, the accepting run is unique for
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
Since every transition in a tagged TA has a unique symbol,
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
have the same internal nodes in the underlying (same) tree.
Moreover, from our definition of TAs, every leaf transition has a unique parent state.
Hence
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
have the same leaf nodes too.
Thus, we have
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
and therefore
T_{1}=T_{2}
.
∎

Theorem C.2 (Theorem 6.6).

The restriction procedure is tag preserving on the tree restriction operation that transforms
T
to
B_{x}\cdot T
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in[n]
. Since the restriction procedures for
\mathcal{A}_{B_{x_{t}}\cdot T}
and
\mathcal{A}_{B_{\overline{x_{t}}}\cdot T}
are symmetric, we will prove the case of
\mathcal{A}_{B_{x_{t}}\cdot T}
and the proof for the later case is similar.

For a given TA
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{R}):=\mathcal{A}_{T}
, let us denote by
\mathcal{A}_{B}=\{Q_{B}=Q\cup Q^{\prime},\Sigma_{B}=\Sigma\cup\{c_{0}\},\Delta_{B}=(\Delta\setminus\Delta_{\mathsf{rm}})\cup\Delta_{\mathsf{add}}\cup\Delta^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{B}=\mathcal{R}\}
the TA
\mathcal{A}_{B_{x_{t}}\cdot T}
constructed from
\mathcal{A}
via our restriction procedure Algorithm
4
. We are going to construct a map

\displaystyle(\cdot)_{B}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{B})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{B}

as follows: for each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with an accepting run
\rho
, since
\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{B}
, we can form a tree
T_{B}
such that
tag(T_{B})=tag(T)
with each internal node
u\in N_{T_{B}}
being such that
L_{T_{B}}(u)=L_{T}(u)\in\Sigma\subseteq\Sigma_{B}
. Let
\rho_{B}
be the run of
T_{B}
defined as

(1)

L_{\rho_{B}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)
;

(2)

for each internal node
u\in N_{T_{B}}
,

L_{\rho_{B}}(u)=\begin{cases}L_{\rho}(u)&\text{ if }L_{T}(u)\text{ has variants in }\{x_{1},\dots,x_{t}\}\text{ as its label}\\
L_{\rho}(u)^{\prime}&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases};

(3)

for each leaf node
u=0.u^{\prime}
or
u=1.u^{\prime}
with an internal node
u^{\prime}
,

L_{\rho_{B}}(u)=\begin{cases}L_{\rho}(u)^{\prime}&\text{ if }L_{\rho_{B}}(u^{\prime})=L_{\rho}(u^{\prime})^{\prime}\\
L_{\rho}(u)^{\prime}&\text{ if }u=0.u^{\prime},L_{\rho_{B}}(u^{\prime})=L_{\rho}(u^{\prime})\text{ and }L_{T}(u^{\prime})\text{ has variants in $x_{t}$ as label}\\
L_{\rho}(u)&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

Then, for every internal node
u\in N_{T_{B}}
, we have

L_{\rho_{B}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to50.55pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.2744pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

for each leaf node
u\in N_{T_{B}}
. Moreover,
L_{\rho_{B}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)\in\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}_{B}
. Thus
R_{B}
is an accepting run of
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{B})
over
T_{B}
and hence
T_{B}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{B})
, i.e., the map
(\cdot)_{B}
is well-defined.

By
Lemma

C.1
, all different trees
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
have different tags and, since
\mathrm{Tag}(T_{B})=\mathrm{Tag}(T)
for each
T
by the construction, we have that all
T_{B}
’s are different as well. It follows that the map
(\cdot)_{B}
is injective with
\mathrm{Tag}(T)=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{B})
.

Claim 3:

The map
(\cdot)_{B}
is surjective.

Proof:
For each
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{B})
with an accepting run
R^{\prime}
, since
\Sigma_{B}=\Sigma\cup\{c_{0}\}
and
c_{0}
corresponds to the leaf transition only, we can form a tree
T
such that
\mathrm{Tag}(T)=\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime})
and, for each internal node
u\in N_{T}
,
L_{T}(u)=L_{T^{\prime}}(u)\in\Sigma_{B}\setminus\{c_{0}\}=\Sigma
. To construct an accepting run
\rho
of
T
such that
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, we first note that the set of root states
\mathcal{R}_{B}
of
\mathcal{A}_{B}
is identical to the one
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore
L_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon)\in\mathcal{R}_{B}=\mathcal{R}
and we may set
L_{\rho}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon)
. For each internal node
u\in N_{T}
, we set

L_{\rho}(u)=\begin{cases}L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)&\text{ if }L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)\in Q\\
(L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u))^{un-primed}&\text{ if }L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)\in Q^{\prime}\end{cases}

where, since
Q^{\prime}
is in one-to-one correspondence to
Q
, we denote by
(q^{\prime})^{un-primed}
the corresponding
q\in Q
to
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
. By construction, all elements in the range of
N_{B}
belong to
Q
. For an internal transition

L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)\text{\small{\hbox to48.15pt{\vbox to13.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.07362pt\lower-4.11372pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

belong to
\Delta
as well by the add/remove step in our procedure. The leaf transitions for the leaf nodes are uniquely determined by their starting states which have been defined. Therefore such an
\rho
is an accepting run of
T
over
\mathcal{A}
, namely,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
. Thus the claim follows.

\blacksquare

Finally, the functionality

\mathrm{UnTag}(T_{B})=B_{x_{t}}\cdot\mathrm{UnTag}(T)

follows directly from the construction of
(\cdot)_{B}
.
∎

Theorem C.3 (Theorem 6.7).

The multiplication procedure over a tagged TA is tag preserving on the tree multiplication operation transforming
T
to
v\cdot T
.

Proof.

The theorem trivially holds because the multiplication procedure only changes the leaf symbols.
∎

Lemma C.4 (Lemma 6.8).

Subtree copying
\mathsf{s.copy_{n}}
is tag-preserving over the tree projection operation
T\rightarrow T_{x_{n}}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,\Delta,\mathcal{R})
be a tagged TA and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=(Q^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}):=\mathsf{s.copy_{n}}(\mathcal{A})
be the TA obtained after subtree copying procedure on
x_{n}
. Note that, by construction,
Q^{\prime}=Q,\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma,\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}
. Let us construct a map

\displaystyle(\cdot)_{x_{n}}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hfill T

\displaystyle\mapsto T_{x_{n}}

as follows: for each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with its accepting run
\rho
, we form a
tree
T_{x_{n}}
such that
\mathrm{Tag}(T_{x_{n}})=\mathrm{Tag}(T)
with each internal node
u\in N_{T_{x_{n}}}
being such that
L_{T_{x_{n}}}(u)=L_{T}(u)\in\Sigma
. Let
\rho_{x_{n}}
be the run of
T_{x_{n}}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
defined as

(1)

L_{\rho_{x_{n}}}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho}(\epsilon)
;

(2)

L_{\rho_{x_{n}}}(u)=L_{\rho}(u)
for each internal node
u\in N_{T_{x_{n}}}
except those nodes in the
x_{n}
’s layer;

(3)

and for the remaining internal nodes
u^{\prime}=0.u
and
u^{\prime\prime}=1.u
, we set
L_{\rho_{x_{n}}}(0.u)=L_{\rho}(1.u)
and
L_{\rho_{x_{n}}}(1.u)=L_{\rho}(1.u)
;

and for the leaf nodes
u
,
L_{\rho_{x_{n}}}(u)
and
L_{T_{x_{n}}}(u)
are then uniquely determined by the structure of the TA
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. By construction, we have, for
u
in the layer just above the leaf nodes,

Claim 4:

The map
(\cdot)_{x_{n}}
is surjective.

Proof:
For each
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
with the accepting run
\rho^{\prime}
, again since

\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma
, we can form a tree
T
such that
\mathrm{Tag}(T)=\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime})
, and, for each internal node
u\in N_{T_{x_{n}}}
,
L_{T}(u)=L_{T^{\prime}}(u)\in\Sigma
. To construct an accepting run
R
of
T
, note that the set of root states
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is identical to the one of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore we may set
L_{\rho}(\epsilon)=L_{\rho^{\prime}}(\epsilon)\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}
. For each internal node
u\in N_{T}
other than in the
x_{n}
’s layer, we set
L_{\rho}(u)=L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)
. For each internal node
u
in the
x_{n}
’s layer, we set (1)
L_{\rho}(u)=L_{\rho^{\prime}}(u)
, (2)
L_{\rho}(1.u)=L_{\rho^{\prime}}(1.u)
, and (3)

, where

. Those labelings of leaf nodes are then uniquely determined by the structure of the TA. Thus, by construction,
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
and hence the map
(\cdot)_{x_{n}}
is surjective.

\blacksquare

Finally,

\mathrm{UnTag}(T_{x_{n}})=\mathrm{UnTag}(T)_{x_{n}}

follows directly from the construction of
(\cdot)_{x_{n}}
.
∎

Lemma C.5 (Forward and backward swapping preserves quantum states).

Given a TA
\mathcal{A}
that encodes a set of quantum states,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
represent the same set of quantum states.

Proof.

First of all, the binary tree representations of a function
T\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}^{5}
are parametrized by the orders on the variables
x_{i}
’s. Fix
t\in[n]
. We denote by
T
also the binary tree representing the function (as in this whole article) on the standard ordering

(16)

x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots>x_{t}>x_{t+1}>\cdots x_{n}

and by
T^{\prime}
the binary tree representing the same function but on the order

(17)

x_{1}>x_{2}>\cdots x_{t-1}>x_{t+1}>x_{t}>x_{t+2}>\cdots x_{n}.

Such a correspondence between the set of binary trees with respect to standard ordering in
Eq.

16
and the set of trees with respect to the ordering in
Eq.

17
is clearly one-to-one. Let
\mathcal{A}
be the tagged TA consisting of
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the standard order
Eq.

16
. By
Algorithm

7
of the
\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}
procedure, it is clear that
\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
consists of all the
T^{\prime}
and vice versa. Thus there is a one-to-one correspondence
\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
. Moreover, since
T
and its corresponding
T^{\prime}
are by definition representing the same function as well as the same state, we have that the TAs
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
represent the same set of quantum states.

The proof of the case that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
(if
\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}
is applicable) represent the same set of quantum states is in the same fashion.
∎

By the above
Lemma

C.5
, let us define the induced maps
\mathsf{f}_{k}
and
\mathsf{b}_{k}
on tags of trees, namely,
\mathsf{f}_{k}(\mathrm{Tag}(T)):=\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{b}_{k}(\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime})):=\mathrm{Tag}(T)
. It is easy to see that

(18)

\mathrm{Tag}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{k}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{k}(T)))=\mathsf{b}_{k}(\mathrm{Tag}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{k}(T)))=\mathsf{b}_{k}(\mathsf{f}_{k}(\mathrm{Tag}(T)))=\mathrm{Tag}(T).

We also introduce the notion
\mathcal{A}_{1}\simeq_{state}\mathcal{A}_{2}
between two TAs
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
if there is a bijection
S\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
represent the same set of quantum states.

Next, we prove an auxiliary lemma and use it to show that the projection procedure is tag preserving.

Lemma C.6.

Given a TA
\mathcal{A}
. For each
t\in[n]
and for each TA
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
with
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
, we have
\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

By assumption, there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
such that for each
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
and
its corresponding
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
, we have
\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime})=\mathrm{Tag}(T^{\prime\prime})
(and vice versa). We denote by
S^{\prime}(\cdot)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
such a bijection.

Since
\mathcal{A}\simeq_{state}\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A})
by the above Lemma
C.5
, there
exists a bijection
\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\cdot)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))
and similarly there is a bijection

\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\cdot)\colon\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))
. Thus we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}}

and the composition map

S(\cdot):=\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\cdot)\circ S^{\prime}(\cdot)\circ\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(\cdot):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}))

is well-defined and is bijective since all the three maps are bijective. Moreover, for each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tag}(S(T))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tag}(\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(S^{\prime}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(T)))

\displaystyle\quad\text{By Def. of }S

\displaystyle=\mathsf{b}_{t}(\mathrm{Tag}(S^{\prime}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(T)))

\displaystyle\quad\text{By Def. of }\mathsf{b}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{b}_{t}(\mathrm{Tag}(\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}(T))

\displaystyle\quad\text{By assumption of }S^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{b}_{t}(\mathsf{f}_{t}(\mathrm{Tag}(T)))

\displaystyle\quad\text{By Def. of }\mathsf{f}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tag}(T)

\displaystyle\quad\text{By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:swap.state.inv}}.

Thus we have
\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathcal{A}
.
∎

Theorem C.7 (Theorem 6.11).

The projection procedure is tag preserving
with respect to the tree projection operations that transforms a tree
T
to
T_{x}
.

Proof.

By construction, we have the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{l-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{l})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{l-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{l})}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{l-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{s.copy}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}}

where the maps
S_{i}
’s are defined inductively as

(1)

S_{l-1}:=\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}\circ\mathsf{s.copy}_{t}\circ\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}
, and

(2)

S_{i}:=\mathsf{b.swap}_{t}\circ S_{i+1}\circ\mathsf{f.swap}_{t}
for
i=0,1,\dots,l-2
and
S:=S_{0}
.

Since now
x_{t}
’s are in the layer just above leaf transition,
\mathsf{s.copy}_{t}
is tag preserving by
Lemma

C.4
, we have

\mathcal{A}_{l}\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{l}
. Apply
Lemma

C.6
inductively, we
have
\mathcal{A}_{i}\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{i}
for each
i
and hence
\mathcal{A}\simeq_{\mathrm{Tag}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Moreover, since

\mathcal{A}\simeq_{state}\mathcal{A}_{1}\simeq_{state}\cdots\simeq_{state}\mathcal{A}_{l}\text{ and }\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\simeq_{state}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}\simeq_{state}\cdots\simeq_{state}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{l},

the functionality follows from the functionality of
\mathsf{s.copy}_{t}
.
∎

Theorem C.8 (Theorem 6.12).

Given two tagged TAs
\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{T_{2}}
as the input of the binary operation, the resulting TA
\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}\pm T_{2}}
recognizes
\{T_{1}\pm T_{2}\mid T_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}})\land T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{2}})\land\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{2})\}
.

Proof.

It is similar to the proof of Theorem
C.2
that there exists a one-to-one correspondence

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}})\times\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{2}})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}\pm T_{2}})

\displaystyle(T_{1},T_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}T_{1}\pm T_{2}&\text{ if }\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{2})\\
\emptyset&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

and, by the binary operation algorithm, we have
\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1}\pm T_{2})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{2})
for any pair
(T_{1},T_{2})\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{1}})\times\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{T_{2}})
with
\mathrm{Tag}(T_{1})=\mathrm{Tag}(T_{2})
. The functionality follows from the algorithm of leaf transitions (of the binary operation).
∎

Appendix D
The Circuit of Grover’s Search Algorithm with All Possible Oracles

With the operations over the symbolic representation of quantum states
introduced in the main text, we are able to perform all gate operations
in
Table

1
.
In the current section, we will use Grover’s search algorithm
(
Grover 1996
)
to demonstrate how to use these gate operations for automatic verification of the safety properties of quantum algorithms.

The Grover’s search algorithm assumes the existence of an
oracle
that knows the problem of interest and can answer related questions.

Definition D.1 (Phase Oracle).

Given a function
f\colon\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
, a
phase oracle

O_{f}
transforms a quantum state
\left|x\right\rangle
to
(-1)^{f(x)}\left|x\right\rangle
.

Grover’s Problem

Input:

A function
f(x)
that returns
1
if
x=s
for some unique secret string
s\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
0
otherwise, and
a phase oracle
O_{f}
.

Output:

The unique secret string
s
.

\xleftarrow{\hskip 120.92421pt}
one Grover iteration
\xrightarrow{\hskip 120.92421pt}

\left|s_{1}\right\rangle

X

X

X

X

\left|s_{2}\right\rangle

X

X

X

X

\left|s_{3}\right\rangle

X

X

X

X

\left|0\right\rangle

H

H

X

X

X

X

H

H

X

X

X

X

H

H

\left|0\right\rangle

H

H

X

X

X

X

H

H

X

X

X

X

H

H

\left|0\right\rangle

H

H

X

X

X

X

H

H

X

X

Z

Z

X

X

H

H

\left|1\right\rangle

H

H

Figure 4
.
An implementation of Grover’s search algorithm.
The boxed area denotes a multi-control gate, which can be implemented with
Toffoli gates and two additional ancilla qubits.
The

area is the oracle circuit, and the part to the right
of the red separator is one Grover iteration.

In
Fig.

4
, we show the circuit implementing 3-qubit Grover’s search
algorithm together with its oracle circuit.
The oracle circuit takes as input qubits
\left|s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}\right\rangle
and uses them as the only solution to
f(x)
.
For the analysis of the algorithm, we create a TA that accepts the set of quantum basis states
\{\left|s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}0001\right\rangle\mid s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}\in\{0,1\}^{3}\}
and use it as the initial TA.

The circuit to the right of the red separator in
Fig.

4
is called
a
Grover iteration
, whose function is to increase the probability
amplitude of the basis
\left|s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}+\right\rangle
.
The number of gates required per iteration is linear with our encoding and is exponential with an enumeration-based approach.
Intuitively, a Grover iteration increases the probability that the measured outcome of

x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
equals to the solution
s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}
.
It is executed repeatedly for roughly
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{2^{n}})

times in order to obtain a high enough probability that
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}
is measured
to be
s_{1}s_{2}s_{3}
(see
(
Grover 1996
)
for more details).
After that, we check the equivalence of the obtained TA against the reference answer.

Appendix E
Additional Information About the Experiments

The pre-conditions, circuits and post-conditions of each experiment are described as follows. For Bernstein-Vazirani’s algorithm, the parameter
n
is the length of the hidden string.
Fig.

5
is an example circuit from the hidden string
s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}=011
. For each
s_{i}=1
, the corresponding qubit
q_{i}
is attached to the control qubit of a CNOT gate whose target is located at the bottom qubit. In this algorithm, the initial state can only be
|0\rangle
, and the output state has probability 100% at hidden string’s basis state. More precisely, when
(n=m+1)

Pre:

\displaystyle\{\left|0^{n}\right\rangle\}

if

is odd

\displaystyle\{(10)^{m/2}1\}

if

is even.

Notice that for the sake of constructing the post condition, our implementation actually appends one more H gate at the target qubit to the end of the circuit.

Figure 5
.
An implementation of Bernstein-Vazirani’s algorithm. The middle part between the two red separators is the quantum oracle corresponding to the hidden string
s_{2}s_{1}s_{0}=011
.

For Grover’s algorithm with one oracle, the problem size
n
is also the length of the hidden string. The circuit can be easily modified from
Fig.

4
by hardwiring particular X gates from the unique secret string and then removing all
|s_{i}\rangle
qubits. The initial state of the bottom qubit can only be
|1\rangle
and those of other qubits can only be
|0\rangle
. The output state has the highest probability at hidden
string’s basis state if the proper number of iterations is given. For Grover’s algorithm with all possible oracles, the readers can refer to
Appendix

D
. Notice that we still append one more H gate at the target qubit to the end of the circuit here for the sake of constructing the post condition. Besides, the representation below does not assume an interleaving of control qubits and work qubits.

Grover-Single

\displaystyle(n=2m,\ m\geq 3)

Pre:

\displaystyle\{|0^{n}\rangle\}

Post:

\displaystyle\{a_{h}|s0^{m-1}1\rangle+\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}^{m},i\neq s}a_{l}|i0^{m-1}1\rangle\ |\ s=(01)^{(m-1)/2}0\}

if
m
is odd

\displaystyle\{a_{h}|s0^{m-1}1\rangle+\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}^{m},i\neq s}a_{l}|i0^{m-1}1\rangle\ |\ s=(01)^{m/2}\}

if
m
is even.

Grover-All

\displaystyle(n=3m,\ m\geq 3)

Pre:

\displaystyle\{|s0^{m}0^{m}\rangle\ |\ s\in\{0,1\}^{m}\}

Post:

\displaystyle\{a_{h}|ss0^{m-1}1\rangle+\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}^{m},i\neq s}a_{l}|si0^{m-1}1\rangle\ |\ s=\{0,1\}^{m}\}

For multi-controlled Toffoli gates, the problem size
n
is the number of control qubits. Our circuit of multi-controlled Toffoli can be found in
Fig.

6
and the total number of qubits is
2n
. We test this gate with the initial automaton consisting of all basis states that have control qubits and the target qubit
0
or
1
and work qubits only
0
. In this case, the post-condition TA would be the same as the pre-condition TA. The representation below does not assume an interleaving of control qubits and work qubits either.

MCToffoli

\displaystyle(n=2m,\ m\geq 3)

Pre:

\displaystyle\{|c0^{m-1}t\rangle\ |\ c\in\{0,1\}^{m}\land t\in\{0,1\}\}

Post:

\displaystyle\{|c0^{m-1}t\rangle\ |\ c\in\{0,1\}^{m}\land t\in\{0,1\}\}

Multi-controlled Toffoli of 5 control qubits

For randomly generated gates, the problem size is simply the number of qubits. Following
(
Tsai
et al. 2021
)
’s configuration, the ratio
of #qubits : #gates is fixed to 1:3, and there are 10 circuits for each size. The gates and the applied qubits are picked uniformly at random. The initial state consists of only
|0\rangle
.
